</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Liz_Truss_sidebar" title="Template:Liz Truss sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Liz_Truss_sidebar" title="Template talk:Liz Truss sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Liz_Truss_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Mary Elizabeth Truss</b> (born 26 July 1975) is a British politician who served as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a> and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Leader of the Conservative Party (UK)">Leader of the Conservative Party</a> from September to October 2022. On her 50th day in office, she stepped down amid <a href="/wiki/October_2022_United_Kingdom_government_crisis" title="October 2022 United Kingdom government crisis">a government crisis</a>, making her the <a href="/wiki/List_of_prime_ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom_by_length_of_tenure" title="List of prime ministers of the United Kingdom by length of tenure">shortest-serving prime minister</a> in the history of the United Kingdom. Truss previously held various <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Cabinet of the United Kingdom">Cabinet</a> positions under prime ministers <a href="/wiki/David_Cameron" title="David Cameron">David Cameron</a>, <a href="/wiki/Theresa_May" title="Theresa May">Theresa May</a> and <a href="/wiki/Boris_Johnson" title="Boris Johnson">Boris Johnson</a>, lastly as <a href="/wiki/Foreign_Secretary" title="Foreign Secretary">foreign secretary</a> from 2021 to 2022. She has been the <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(United_Kingdom)" title="Member of Parliament (United Kingdom)">member of Parliament</a> (MP) for <a href="/wiki/South_West_Norfolk_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="South West Norfolk (UK Parliament constituency)">South West Norfolk</a> since 2010.
</p><p>Truss studied <a href="/wiki/Philosophy,_politics_and_economics" title="Philosophy, politics and economics">philosophy, politics and economics</a> at <a href="/wiki/Merton_College,_Oxford" title="Merton College, Oxford">Merton College, Oxford</a>, and was the president of <a href="/wiki/Oxford_University_Liberal_Democrats" title="Oxford University Liberal Democrats">Oxford University Liberal Democrats</a>. In 1996, she joined the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative Party</a>. She worked at <a href="/wiki/Shell_plc" title="Shell plc">Royal Dutch Shell</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cable_%26_Wireless_plc" title="Cable &amp; Wireless plc">Cable &amp; Wireless</a> and was the deputy director of the think tank <a href="/wiki/Reform_(think_tank)" title="Reform (think tank)">Reform</a>. After two unsuccessful attempts to be elected to the <a href="/wiki/UK_House_of_Commons" class="mw-redirect" title="UK House of Commons">House of Commons</a>, she became the MP for South West Norfolk at the <a href="/wiki/2010_UK_general_election" class="mw-redirect" title="2010 UK general election">2010 general election</a>. As a backbencher, she called for reform in several policy areas including childcare, mathematics education and the economy. Truss co-founded the <a href="/wiki/Free_Enterprise_Group" title="Free Enterprise Group">Free Enterprise Group</a> of  Conservative MPs and wrote or co-wrote a number of papers and books, including <i><a href="/wiki/After_the_Coalition" title="After the Coalition">After the Coalition</a></i> and <i><a href="/wiki/Britannia_Unchained" title="Britannia Unchained">Britannia Unchained</a></i>.
</p><p>Truss was the <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_Under-Secretary_of_State_for_Childcare_and_Education" class="mw-redirect" title="Parliamentary Under-Secretary of State for Childcare and Education">parliamentary under-secretary of state for childcare and education</a> from 2012 to 2014 before Cameron appointed her <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_the_Environment,_Food_and_Rural_Affairs" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for the Environment, Food and Rural Affairs">secretary of state for the environment, food and rural affairs</a> in <a href="/wiki/2014_British_cabinet_reshuffle" title="2014 British cabinet reshuffle">a cabinet reshuffle</a>. Although she campaigned for the UK to remain in the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a>, Truss supported <a href="/wiki/Brexit" title="Brexit">Brexit</a> following the outcome of the <a href="/wiki/2016_United_Kingdom_European_Union_membership_referendum" title="2016 United Kingdom European Union membership referendum">2016 referendum</a>. Following Cameron's resignation in 2016, his successor <a href="/wiki/Theresa_May" title="Theresa May">Theresa May</a> appointed her as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Justice_(UK)" title="Secretary of State for Justice (UK)">secretary of state for justice</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lord_Chancellor" title="Lord Chancellor">lord chancellor</a>, making Truss the first woman to serve as lord chancellor in the office's thousand-year history; in the aftermath of the <a href="/wiki/2017_UK_general_election" class="mw-redirect" title="2017 UK general election">2017 general election</a>, she was demoted to <a href="/wiki/Chief_Secretary_to_the_Treasury" title="Chief Secretary to the Treasury">chief secretary to the Treasury</a>. After May announced her resignation in May 2019, Truss supported Johnson's successful bid to become Conservative leader and prime minister. He appointed Truss as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_International_Trade" title="Secretary of State for International Trade">secretary of state for international trade</a> and <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Board_of_Trade" title="President of the Board of Trade">president of the Board of Trade</a> in July; she was subsequently given the additional role of <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Women_and_Equalities" title="Minister for Women and Equalities">minister for women and equalities</a> in September. Johnson promoted Truss to foreign secretary in the <a href="/wiki/2021_British_cabinet_reshuffle" title="2021 British cabinet reshuffle">2021 cabinet reshuffle</a>; during her time in the position, she led negotiations on the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Protocol" title="Northern Ireland Protocol">Northern Ireland Protocol</a> and the UK's response to the <a href="/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine" title="Russian invasion of Ukraine">Russian invasion of Ukraine</a>.
</p><p>In 2022, Truss defeated <a href="/wiki/Rishi_Sunak" title="Rishi Sunak">Rishi Sunak</a> in <a href="/wiki/July%E2%80%93September_2022_Conservative_Party_leadership_election" title="July–September 2022 Conservative Party leadership election">a leadership election</a> to succeed Johnson, who had resigned because of an <a href="/wiki/July_2022_United_Kingdom_government_crisis" title="July 2022 United Kingdom government crisis">earlier government crisis</a>, and was appointed as prime minister by <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">Elizabeth II</a> two days before <a href="/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Elizabeth_II" title="Death and state funeral of Elizabeth II">the monarch's death</a>; her government's business was subsequently suspended during a <a href="/wiki/National_day_of_mourning" title="National day of mourning">national mourning period</a> of 10 days. In response to the <a href="/wiki/2021%E2%80%93present_United_Kingdom_cost-of-living_crisis" title="2021–present United Kingdom cost-of-living crisis">rising cost of living</a> and <a href="/wiki/2021_United_Kingdom_natural_gas_supplier_crisis" title="2021 United Kingdom natural gas supplier crisis">increased energy prices</a>, <a href="/wiki/Truss_ministry" title="Truss ministry">her ministry</a> announced the <a href="/wiki/Energy_Price_Guarantee" class="mw-redirect" title="Energy Price Guarantee">Energy Price Guarantee</a>. The government then announced <a href="/wiki/September_2022_United_Kingdom_mini-budget" title="September 2022 United Kingdom mini-budget">large-scale tax cuts and borrowing</a>, which led to financial instability and were largely reversed. Facing mounting criticism and loss of confidence in her leadership, Truss announced her resignation as leader of the Conservative Party on 20 October. Sunak was <a href="/wiki/October_2022_Conservative_Party_leadership_election" title="October 2022 Conservative Party leadership election">elected unopposed</a> as her successor, and succeeded her as prime minister five days later. She remains in the House of Commons as a <a href="/wiki/Backbencher" title="Backbencher">backbencher</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_education">Early life and education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liz_Truss&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life and education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Merton_College,_Oxford_from_Merton_Field.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Merton_College%2C_Oxford_from_Merton_Field.jpg/200px-Merton_College%2C_Oxford_from_Merton_Field.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="112" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Merton_College%2C_Oxford_from_Merton_Field.jpg/300px-Merton_College%2C_Oxford_from_Merton_Field.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Merton_College%2C_Oxford_from_Merton_Field.jpg/400px-Merton_College%2C_Oxford_from_Merton_Field.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3143" data-file-height="1756" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Merton_College,_Oxford" title="Merton College, Oxford">Merton College, Oxford</a>, where Truss studied (pictured in 2023)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Mary Elizabeth Truss was born on 26 July 1975 at the <a href="/wiki/John_Radcliffe_Hospital" title="John Radcliffe Hospital">John Radcliffe Hospital</a> in <a href="/wiki/Oxford" title="Oxford">Oxford</a>, England.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale20225_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale20225-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> She was the second child of <a href="/wiki/John_Truss" title="John Truss">John</a> and Priscilla Truss (<span title="Name at birth"><a href="/wiki/Birth_name#Maiden_and_married_names" title="Birth name">née</a>&#160;Grasby</span>); the year prior to Truss's birth, their first son, Matthew, had died.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale20225_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale20225-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> Truss was known by her middle name, <i>Elizabeth</i>, since early childhood with her father, a professor of <a href="/wiki/Pure_mathematics" title="Pure mathematics">pure mathematics</a> at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Leeds" title="University of Leeds">University of Leeds</a>, using it on a regular basis, which she preferred;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale20225_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale20225-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBelam2022_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBelam2022-7">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> after being given a badge with "Mary" on it on her first day of school, Truss had asked her teacher that it be changed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale20229_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale20229-8">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Truss later described her parents' politics as being "to the left of <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour</a>";<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEllery2022_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEllery2022-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> her mother, a nurse, was a member of the <a href="/wiki/Campaign_for_Nuclear_Disarmament" title="Campaign for Nuclear Disarmament">Campaign for Nuclear Disarmament</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Profile:_Elizabeth_Truss&quot;._The_Sunday_Times_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Profile:_Elizabeth_Truss&quot;._The_Sunday_Times-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAsthana2012_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsthana2012-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> When Truss stood for election to Parliament as a Conservative in 2010, her mother agreed to campaign with her, but her father declined to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEForsyth2012_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEForsyth2012-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202239_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202239-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Her parents divorced in 2003.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGryllsNorfolkWace2022_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGryllsNorfolkWace2022-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202241_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202241-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1062260506">.mw-parser-output .quotebox{background-color:#F9F9F9;border:1px solid #aaa;box-sizing:border-box;padding:10px;font-size:88%;max-width:100%}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft{margin:.5em 1.4em .8em 0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright{margin:.5em 0 .8em 1.4em}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.centered{overflow:hidden;position:relative;margin:.5em auto .8em auto}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft span,.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright span{font-style:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox>blockquote{margin:0;padding:0;border-left:0;font-family:inherit;font-size:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-title{background-color:#F9F9F9;text-align:center;font-size:110%;font-weight:bold}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote>:first-child{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote:last-child>:last-child{margin-bottom:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:before{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" “ ";vertical-align:-45%;line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:after{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" ” ";line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .left-aligned{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .right-aligned{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .center-aligned{text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quote-title,.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quotebox-quote{display:block}.mw-parser-output .quotebox cite{display:block;font-style:normal}@media screen and (max-width:640px){.mw-parser-output .quotebox{width:100%!important;margin:0 0 .8em!important;float:none!important}}</style><div class="quotebox pullquote floatleft" style="width:200px; ; background-color: #E0E0F8;">
<blockquote class="quotebox-quote left-aligned" style="">
<p>[S]he stands out in my memory as a sort of strange, unfocused force, hugely in favour of action and change<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>... it was always hard to see the aim of it all, or where it might lead, except that she would be at the center of it.
</p>
</blockquote>
<p><cite class="right-aligned" style=""><a href="/wiki/Julian_Glover_(journalist)" title="Julian Glover (journalist)">Julian Glover</a> on Truss, his former schoolmate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcGee2022a_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGee2022a-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup></cite>
</p>
</div>
<p>In 1977 Truss and her parents moved to Warsaw, Poland, but returned to Britain after John and Priscilla found it "grim".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale20227–8_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale20227–8-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> After living briefly in <a href="/wiki/Kidderminster" title="Kidderminster">Kidderminster</a>, <a href="/wiki/Worcestershire" title="Worcestershire">Worcestershire</a>, the family moved to <a href="/wiki/Paisley,_Renfrewshire" title="Paisley, Renfrewshire">Paisley, Renfrewshire</a> in Scotland, when Truss was four years old, with Truss attending West Primary School.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWheelerFrancis2022_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheelerFrancis2022-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale20228–9_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale20228–9-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> In 1985 they moved south to <a href="/wiki/Leeds" title="Leeds">Leeds</a>, where Truss attended <a href="/wiki/Roundhay_School" title="Roundhay School">Roundhay School</a>; she later said in 2022 that at the school she "saw kids<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>... being let down", a claim which was criticised as inaccurate by former Roundhay pupils.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202211–13_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202211–13-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVinter2022_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVinter2022-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPengelly2022_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPengelly2022-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> When Truss was 12 she and her family spent a year in <a href="/wiki/Burnaby" title="Burnaby">Burnaby, British Columbia</a>, where she attended Parkcrest Elementary School whilst her father taught at <a href="/wiki/Simon_Fraser_University" title="Simon Fraser University">Simon Fraser University</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWoods2022_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoods2022-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Truss praised the Canadian curriculum and the attitude that it was "really good to be top of the class", which she contrasted with her education at Roundhay.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAsthana2012_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsthana2012-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> A classmate recalled Truss as being "very smart" and that she was "confident, chatty, [and] tried to get to know everyone".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202211–12_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202211–12-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Truss's parents had initially wanted her to study at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Cambridge" title="University of Cambridge">University of Cambridge</a>, but Truss instead elected to go to <a href="/wiki/University_of_Oxford" title="University of Oxford">Oxford</a> in what Cole and Heale call a "bout of teenage rebellion".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202217_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202217-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> She applied to <a href="/wiki/Merton_College,_Oxford" title="Merton College, Oxford">Merton College</a> but was instead listed as a candidate for the all-women's <a href="/wiki/St_Hilda%27s_College,_Oxford" title="St Hilda&#39;s College, Oxford">St Hilda's College</a>, to her dismay; she then complained to both colleges, after which she was accepted by Merton, beginning her studies there in 1993.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202217–18_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202217–18-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Truss read <a href="/wiki/Philosophy,_politics_and_economics" title="Philosophy, politics and economics">philosophy, politics and economics</a> and graduated in 1996.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcSmith2014b_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcSmith2014b-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> During her time at university, Truss was active in the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_(UK)" title="Liberal Democrats (UK)">Liberal Democrats</a>. She became the president of the <a href="/wiki/Oxford_University_Liberal_Democrats" title="Oxford University Liberal Democrats">Oxford University Liberal Democrats</a> in her first year<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202224_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202224-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> and a member of the national executive committee of <a href="/wiki/Young_Liberals_(UK)" title="Young Liberals (UK)">Liberal Democrat Youth and Students</a> (LDYS) in 1995.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202227_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202227-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> During Truss's previous unsuccessful bid for the LDYS executive, the party's leader, <a href="/wiki/Paddy_Ashdown" title="Paddy Ashdown">Paddy Ashdown</a>, said she was "a good debater and is utterly fearless".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202227_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202227-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> During her time as a Liberal Democrat, Truss supported the <a href="/wiki/Legality_of_cannabis" title="Legality of cannabis">legalisation of cannabis</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Republicanism_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Republicanism in the United Kingdom">abolition of the monarchy</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESyalSinmazQuinnWalker2022_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESyalSinmazQuinnWalker2022-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202228_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202228-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> and campaigned against the <a href="/wiki/Criminal_Justice_and_Public_Order_Act_1994" title="Criminal Justice and Public Order Act 1994">Criminal Justice and Public Order Act 1994</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESteerpike2022_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESteerpike2022-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> However, by November 1995, Truss had become critical of the Liberal Democrats, as she "realised the Tory Party was saying quite sane things"; in her last year at the university, she resigned from the LDYS.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202228_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202228-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> By 1996, Truss had joined the Conservative Party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202232_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202232-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Career">Career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liz_Truss&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>From 1996 to 2000 Truss worked for <a href="/wiki/Shell_plc" title="Shell plc">Royal Dutch Shell</a>, living in <a href="/wiki/Lewisham" title="Lewisham">Lewisham</a> and <a href="/wiki/Greenwich" title="Greenwich">Greenwich</a> and qualifying as a <a href="/wiki/Chartered_Institute_of_Management_Accountants" title="Chartered Institute of Management Accountants">Chartered Management Accountant</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEQuinn2022a_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuinn2022a-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;The_Rt_Hon_Elizabeth_Truss_MP&quot;._gov.uk_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;The_Rt_Hon_Elizabeth_Truss_MP&quot;._gov.uk-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> In 2000, Truss was employed by <a href="/wiki/Cable_%26_Wireless_plc" title="Cable &amp; Wireless plc">Cable &amp; Wireless</a> and rose to the position of economic director before leaving in 2005;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParkerHughes2022_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParkerHughes2022-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> one of her colleagues there, the Labour peer <a href="/wiki/George_Robertson,_Baron_Robertson_of_Port_Ellen" title="George Robertson, Baron Robertson of Port Ellen">George Robertson</a>, said that Truss "had a passion for politics<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>... she [was] fresh minded, enthusiastic and the Tory Party needed people like that".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202242_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202242-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 2008, after losing her first two elections, Truss became the deputy director of <a href="/wiki/Reform_(think_tank)" title="Reform (think tank)">Reform</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Centre-right_politics" title="Centre-right politics">centre-right</a> <a href="/wiki/Think_tank" title="Think tank">think tank</a>, where she advocated for greater effort on countering serious and <a href="/wiki/Organized_crime" title="Organized crime">organised crime</a>, higher standards in schools and action to tackle what Reform perceived as the UK's "falling competitiveness".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEQuinn2022a_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuinn2022a-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202251_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202251-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> She co-authored <i>The Value of Mathematics</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202251_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202251-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> <i>Fit for Purpose</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaldenbyParsonsRosenTruss2009_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaldenbyParsonsRosenTruss2009-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> <i>A New Level</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202251_37-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202251-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> <i>Back To Black</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBassettBosanquetHaldenbyNolan2009_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBassettBosanquetHaldenbyNolan2009-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrierson2022a_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrierson2022a-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> and other reports.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202251_37-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202251-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Council_candidatures">Council candidatures</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liz_Truss&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Council candidatures">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Whilst working at Shell, Truss served as the chair of the <a href="/wiki/Lewisham_Deptford_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Lewisham Deptford (UK Parliament constituency)">Lewisham Deptford</a> <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Association" title="Conservative Association">Conservative Association</a> from 1998 to 2000, having been introduced to the branch by her friend <a href="/wiki/Jackie_Doyle-Price" title="Jackie Doyle-Price">Jackie Doyle-Price</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202232_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202232-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;BBC_Democracy_Live:_Elizabeth_Truss_MP&quot;._BBC_News_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;BBC_Democracy_Live:_Elizabeth_Truss_MP&quot;._BBC_News-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> During this time, at a reception at the <a href="/wiki/Greenwich_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Greenwich (UK Parliament constituency)">Greenwich</a> Conservative Association, Truss met her future husband, <a href="/wiki/Hugh_O%27Leary" title="Hugh O&#39;Leary">Hugh O'Leary</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202232_32-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202232-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> with whom she has two daughters: Frances (born 2006) and Liberty (born 2008).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERumbelow2022_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERumbelow2022-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Truss unsuccessfully stood for election twice in <a href="/wiki/Greenwich_London_Borough_Council" title="Greenwich London Borough Council">Greenwich London Borough Council</a>: for <a href="/wiki/1998_Greenwich_London_Borough_Council_election#Vanbrugh" title="1998 Greenwich London Borough Council election">Vanbrugh ward in 1998</a> and <a href="/wiki/2002_Greenwich_London_Borough_Council_election#Blackheath_Westcombe" title="2002 Greenwich London Borough Council election">Blackheath Westcombe in 2002</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMinorsGrenham1998_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinorsGrenham1998-43">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202240_and_48_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202240_and_48-44">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> The deputy leader of Greenwich Conservatives, Graeme Coombes, recalled in 2022 that "[Truss] said [in 1998] she was hoping to stand for Parliament<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>... she was destined for bigger and better things".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202234_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202234-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> However, Alex Grant, the candidate who had defeated Truss in 2002, called her "largely invisible during the campaign".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202241_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202241-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> After two failed attempts running for Greenwich London Borough Council, Truss was elected for <a href="/wiki/Eltham" title="Eltham">Eltham</a> South in <a href="/wiki/2006_Greenwich_London_Borough_Council_election#Eltham_South" title="2006 Greenwich London Borough Council election">the 2006 council election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETeale2022_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETeale2022-46">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Truss did not seek re-election to the council in 2010, standing down the day she became an MP.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202268_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202268-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Parliamentary_candidatures">Parliamentary candidatures</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liz_Truss&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Parliamentary candidatures">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:David_Cameron_official.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A close-up photograph of David Cameron" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/David_Cameron_official.jpg/180px-David_Cameron_official.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="240" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/David_Cameron_official.jpg/270px-David_Cameron_official.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/David_Cameron_official.jpg/360px-David_Cameron_official.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1440" data-file-height="1920" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/David_Cameron" title="David Cameron">David Cameron</a>, leader of the Conservative Party from 2005 to 2016<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanor2022573_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanor2022573-48">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>At the <a href="/wiki/2001_UK_general_election" class="mw-redirect" title="2001 UK general election">2001 general election</a> Truss was selected for the <a href="/wiki/Safe_seat" title="Safe seat">safe</a> Labour seat of <a href="/wiki/Hemsworth_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Hemsworth (UK Parliament constituency)">Hemsworth</a>, <a href="/wiki/West_Yorkshire" title="West Yorkshire">West Yorkshire</a>, coming a distant second but achieving a 3.2 per cent <a href="/wiki/Swing_(politics)" title="Swing (politics)">swing</a> to the Conservatives, which was considered impressive by her party colleagues.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202239_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202239-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcSmith2014a_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcSmith2014a-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Following the election, which saw the Conservatives make a net gain of one seat and the resignation of the party leader <a href="/wiki/William_Hague" title="William Hague">William Hague</a> because of the "disappointing" result,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBennie2002230_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBennie2002230-50">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Truss supported the former <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Defence" title="Secretary of State for Defence">defence secretary</a> <a href="/wiki/Michael_Portillo" title="Michael Portillo">Michael Portillo</a>'s unsuccessful leadership campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202240_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202240-51">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 2005 Sue Catling, the parliamentary candidate for the <a href="/wiki/Calder_Valley_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Calder Valley (UK Parliament constituency)">Calder Valley</a> constituency, was forced to resign by the local Conservative Association because of an affair with the association's chairman;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStokes2005_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokes2005-52">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Catling claimed that the members of the party that had opposed her were sexist and said that she was "accused of everything except murder and paedophilia".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202243_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202243-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Truss, who was selected as the candidate for the seat, <a href="/wiki/Calder_Valley_(UK_Parliament_constituency)#2005_election" title="Calder Valley (UK Parliament constituency)">narrowly lost</a> to the Labour incumbent after a Conservative campaign which <i><a href="/wiki/The_Yorkshire_Post" title="The Yorkshire Post">The Yorkshire Post</a></i> described as "<a href="/wiki/Blitzkrieg" title="Blitzkrieg">Blitzkreig</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202244–46_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202244–46-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Beginning in 2004, Truss embarked on an 18-month affair with the Conservative MP <a href="/wiki/Mark_Field" title="Mark Field">Mark Field</a>, which ended shortly after the following year's election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202245_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202245-55">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the 2005 election <a href="/wiki/David_Cameron" title="David Cameron">David Cameron</a> <a href="/wiki/2005_Conservative_Party_leadership_election" title="2005 Conservative Party leadership election">replaced</a> <a href="/wiki/Michael_Howard" title="Michael Howard">Michael Howard</a> as leader, and Truss was added to <a href="/wiki/Conservative_A-List" title="Conservative A-List">the party's A-List</a>, a list of promising potential candidates;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcSmith2014a_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcSmith2014a-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> in October 2009 she was selected for the constituency of <a href="/wiki/South_West_Norfolk_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="South West Norfolk (UK Parliament constituency)">South West Norfolk</a> by members of the local Conservative Association, winning over 50 per cent of the vote in the first round of the final against five other candidates, including the future deputy prime minister <a href="/wiki/Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_Coffey" title="Thérèse Coffey">Thérèse Coffey</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHope2009_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHope2009-56">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202254_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202254-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Shortly after her selection, some members of the constituency association objected to Truss's selection because of her failure to declare her affair with Field.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGosden2009_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGosden2009-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> <i><a href="/wiki/The_Mail_on_Sunday" title="The Mail on Sunday">The Mail on Sunday</a></i> was the first to report on the affair, and party members claimed to have been misled over Truss's "<a href="/wiki/Skeleton_in_the_cupboard" class="mw-redirect" title="Skeleton in the cupboard">skeleton in the cupboard</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202254_57-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202254-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> A motion was proposed to terminate Truss's candidature; the proponents of Truss's deselection were branded the "Turnip <a href="/wiki/Taliban" title="Taliban">Taliban</a>" by Conservative Party officials and the press, including by the <i>Mail</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202256_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202256-59">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> a reference to stereotypes about Norfolk being a county of farmers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESyalSinmazQuinnWalker2022_29-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESyalSinmazQuinnWalker2022-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> There was also controversy over the fact that Truss was not from Norfolk, with some in the association asking for a local candidate and saying that she had been "parachuted in".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Former_&#39;Turnip_Taliban&#39;_dissenter_now_backing_Truss_to_be_PM&quot;._Eastern_Daily_Press_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Former_&#39;Turnip_Taliban&#39;_dissenter_now_backing_Truss_to_be_PM&quot;._Eastern_Daily_Press-60">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> On 16 November, the motion was put to the association: following both sides making their arguments, including an "impassioned" speech from Truss, it was defeated by 132 votes to 37.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Tory_woman_wins_selection_battle&quot;._BBC_News_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Tory_woman_wins_selection_battle&quot;._BBC_News-61">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202264_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202264-62">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Backbencher_.282010.E2.80.932012.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Backbencher_(2010–2012)">Backbencher (2010–2012)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liz_Truss&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Backbencher (2010–2012)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Truss was elected as an MP in the <a href="/wiki/2010_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="2010 United Kingdom general election">2010 general election</a>, which saw 148 other Conservatives become MPs for the first time;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJonesNorton2014215_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJonesNorton2014215-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> many of what <i><a href="/wiki/The_Independent_(East_Hampton)" title="The Independent (East Hampton)">The Independent</a></i> described as the "golden generation" would later reach a high rank in government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrice2014_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrice2014-64">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> The Conservatives did not reach an <a href="/wiki/Overall_majority" class="mw-redirect" title="Overall majority">overall majority</a> in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> and entered into <a href="/wiki/Cameron%E2%80%93Clegg_coalition" title="Cameron–Clegg coalition">a coalition government</a> with the Liberal Democrats, with Cameron becoming prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERogers2010_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERogers2010-65">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Following her election to Parliament, Truss campaigned for issues relating to her constituency, including the retention of the <a href="/wiki/Tornado_GR4" class="mw-redirect" title="Tornado GR4">Tornado GR4</a> airbase at <a href="/wiki/RAF_Marham" title="RAF Marham">RAF Marham</a> in her constituency;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Campaign_aim_to_keep_Tornado_base_at_RAF_Marham&quot;._BBC_News_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Campaign_aim_to_keep_Tornado_base_at_RAF_Marham&quot;._BBC_News-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcGurran2014_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGurran2014-67">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> the replacement of the old aircraft with around 150 new <a href="/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II" title="Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II">F-35 strike fighters</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202271_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202271-68">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> the conversion of the <a href="/wiki/A11_road_(England)" title="A11 road (England)">A11</a> west of <a href="/wiki/Thetford" title="Thetford">Thetford</a> into a <a href="/wiki/Dual_carriageway#United_Kingdom" title="Dual carriageway">dual carriageway</a>, which was completed in 2014;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;New_date_for_work_on_dualling_A11_in_Suffolk_and_Norfolk&quot;._BBC_News_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;New_date_for_work_on_dualling_A11_in_Suffolk_and_Norfolk&quot;._BBC_News-69">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBond2014_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBond2014-70">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> and preventing a waste incinerator being built in <a href="/wiki/King%27s_Lynn" title="King&#39;s Lynn">King's Lynn</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcGurran2014_67-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGurran2014-67">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Truss co-founded the <a href="/wiki/Free_Enterprise_Group" title="Free Enterprise Group">Free Enterprise Group</a> (FEG)—a grouping of over 30 <a href="/wiki/Thatcherism" title="Thatcherism">Thatcherite</a> Conservative MPs—in October 2011; the month prior, she had co-authored <i><a href="/wiki/After_the_Coalition" title="After the Coalition">After the Coalition</a></i> with some of the people that would later join the FEG: <a href="/wiki/Priti_Patel" title="Priti Patel">Priti Patel</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kwasi_Kwarteng" title="Kwasi Kwarteng">Kwasi Kwarteng</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dominic_Raab" title="Dominic Raab">Dominic Raab</a> and <a href="/wiki/Chris_Skidmore" title="Chris Skidmore">Chris Skidmore</a>. The book advocated for a number of policies, including a reduction in the top rate of tax to 40 pence per pound and the introduction of a <a href="/wiki/Carbon_tax" title="Carbon tax">carbon tax</a> to reduce pollution.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202277_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202277-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> On the publication, Truss wrote:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Our message must be that the state cannot do everything: while the government can help, it can never fully solve any individual's problems. The <a href="/wiki/NHS" class="mw-redirect" title="NHS">NHS</a> can't keep you healthy if you don't eat or exercise properly. A teacher can't get you the grades if you aren’t prepared to work. The <a href="/wiki/Job_centre" class="mw-redirect" title="Job centre">job centre</a> can't find you work if you aren’t prepared to write a CV.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETruss2011_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETruss2011-72">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Another book by the same authors, <i><a href="/wiki/Britannia_Unchained" title="Britannia Unchained">Britannia Unchained</a></i>, was published in September 2012. The book attracted controversy for claiming that "the British are among the worst idlers in the world. We work among the lowest hours, we retire early and our productivity is poor".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202282–83_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202282–83-73">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> In 2022, Truss stated that the authors had each written a different chapter of the book; Raab had written the chapter which contained that claim.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202282–83_73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202282–83-73">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Truss soon became well known amongst members of parliament in Norfolk for her frequent <a href="/wiki/Photo_op" title="Photo op">photo ops</a> but was well respected amongst Conservative MPs, who recognised her as dedicated and hard-working.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202272–74_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202272–74-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> One of her staff members said that Truss's "attention to the local stuff was just superb".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202272_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202272-75">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Truss advocated for more rigorous teaching in school subjects, especially mathematics,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcGurran2012_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGurran2012-76">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> calling for mathematics lessons to be compulsory for all students until the age of 18 and expressing concern about a perceived overreliance on calculators from <a href="/wiki/Primary_school" title="Primary school">primary school</a> pupils;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECoughlan2012_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoughlan2012-77">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcGurran2011_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGurran2011-78">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> some of her earliest contributions to parliamentary discourse were on the subject of education.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202274_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202274-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Early in her tenure as justice secretary, in response to a question on what advice she would give young women, she replied simply "be good at maths".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022135_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022135-80">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Truss criticised "[giving] <a href="/wiki/Media_studies" title="Media studies">media studies</a> the same value as <a href="/wiki/Further_Mathematics" title="Further Mathematics">further maths</a>" and suggested in 2011 that students should have to sit <a href="/wiki/General_Certificate_of_Secondary_Education" title="General Certificate of Secondary Education">GCSEs</a> for "five traditional academic subjects".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202274–75_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202274–75-81">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Education_under-secretary_.282012.E2.80.932014.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Education_under-secretary_(2012–2014)">Education under-secretary (2012–2014)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liz_Truss&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Education under-secretary (2012–2014)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1062260506"><div class="quotebox pullquote floatright" style="width:220px; ; background-color: #E0E0F8;">
<blockquote class="quotebox-quote left-aligned" style="">
<p>[Y]ou're a great minister, I loved what you did, but we really couldn't go ahead with this one. You're one of the first ministers I've appointed to do something and you've just done it.
</p>
</blockquote>
<p><cite class="left-aligned" style="">David Cameron speaking to Truss about her childcare proposal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202298_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202298-82">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup></cite>
</p>
</div>
<p>In September 2012 Truss was appointed as parliamentary under-secretary of state for education and stepped back from the leadership of the FEG, with Kwarteng taking her place.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202285_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202285-83">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Truss was pleased with her appointment, and praised <a href="/wiki/Michael_Gove" title="Michael Gove">Michael Gove</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Education" title="Secretary of State for Education">secretary of state</a> for the department; she also formed a friendly rivalry with the future <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Health_and_Social_Care" title="Secretary of State for Health and Social Care">health secretary</a> <a href="/wiki/Matt_Hancock" title="Matt Hancock">Matt Hancock</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202286_and_90_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202286_and_90-84">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> In January 2013, Truss wrote a <a href="/wiki/White_paper" title="White paper">white paper</a>—<i>More Great Childcare</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWadsworth2022_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWadsworth2022-85">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>—which proposed increasing the maximum number of children <a href="/wiki/Nanny" title="Nanny">childminders</a> could look after at a time from three to four, intended to reduce the childcare costs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202291–92_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202291–92-86">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWintourMalik2013_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWintourMalik2013-87">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> The press, including Conservative-leaning papers like <i><a href="/wiki/The_Daily_Telegraph" title="The Daily Telegraph">The Daily Telegraph</a></i> and <i><a href="/wiki/The_Times" title="The Times">The Times</a></i>, were largely hostile to the plan; the former claimed that prices would not fall, with the <i><a href="/wiki/The_Guardian" title="The Guardian">Guardian</a></i> columnist <a href="/wiki/Polly_Toynbee" title="Polly Toynbee">Polly Toynbee</a> challenging Truss to demonstrate how to care for so many children on her own.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEToynbee2013_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEToynbee2013-88">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJowit2013_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJowit2013-89">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Following a negative response from trade unions and childminders, Truss was called into a meeting with the deputy prime minister <a href="/wiki/Nick_Clegg" title="Nick Clegg">Nick Clegg</a>, who told her that "some of this is fine" but the maximum childminder increase went "much too far" and advised her to revise the proposal;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202294_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202294-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Truss ignored Clegg and pushed ahead with the plan, angering Clegg who then blocked the proposals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202295_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202295-91">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDominiczak2013_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDominiczak2013-92">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStratton2013_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStratton2013-93">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Truss also announced proposals to reform <a href="/wiki/A-level" title="A-level">A-levels</a> by concentrating exams at the end of two-year courses and said that the UK should attempt to "out-educate" countries in Asia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202298_82-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202298-82">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;A-level_shake_up_will_&#39;end_the_treadmill&#39;_of_repeated_exams&quot;._BBC_News_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;A-level_shake_up_will_&#39;end_the_treadmill&#39;_of_repeated_exams&quot;._BBC_News-94">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMason2014_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMason2014-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Environment_secretary_.282014.E2.80.932016.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Environment_secretary_(2014–2016)">Environment secretary (2014–2016)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liz_Truss&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Environment secretary (2014–2016)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Elizabeth_truss2014.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Truss facing frontward, with long blonde hair and blue clothes in front of a light grey background." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Elizabeth_truss2014.jpg/180px-Elizabeth_truss2014.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="270" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Elizabeth_truss2014.jpg/270px-Elizabeth_truss2014.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Elizabeth_truss2014.jpg/360px-Elizabeth_truss2014.jpg 2x" data-file-width="427" data-file-height="640" /></a><figcaption>Official portrait, 2014</figcaption></figure>
<p>In July 2014 during <a href="/wiki/2014_British_cabinet_reshuffle" title="2014 British cabinet reshuffle">a cabinet reshuffle</a>, Truss was appointed <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Environment,_Food_and_Rural_Affairs" title="Secretary of State for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs">secretary of state for environment, food and rural affairs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Reshuffle_at-a-glance:_In,_out_and_moved_about&quot;._BBC_News_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Reshuffle_at-a-glance:_In,_out_and_moved_about&quot;._BBC_News-96">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Truss was originally to be made a minister of state, but Cameron changed his mind on the morning of the reshuffle.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022104_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022104-97">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Truss's predecessor <a href="/wiki/Owen_Paterson" title="Owen Paterson">Owen Paterson</a> "stormed out" of Cameron's Commons study when told he was to be dismissed; nevertheless, he gave her his phone number and offered his support.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022105–106_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022105–106-98">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> Paterson was dismissed partly because of his <a href="/wiki/Badger_cull#Proposed_2014/15_cull" class="mw-redirect" title="Badger cull">culling plans for badgers with tuberculosis</a>, which Truss later supported.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPayne202212_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPayne202212-99">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022105_and_109_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022105_and_109-100">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> Truss's early actions at the department included setting up a "food crime unit" to prevent incidents similar to the <a href="/wiki/2013_horse_meat_scandal" title="2013 horse meat scandal">2013 horse meat scandal</a>, approving planning for the <a href="/wiki/Thames_Tideway_Scheme" title="Thames Tideway Scheme">Thames Tideway Tunnel</a>, and development of <a href="/wiki/Flood_Re" title="Flood Re">Flood Re</a>, a scheme designed to protect homes at a high risk of flooding.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022109_and_111_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022109_and_111-101">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> Truss was later remarked by an aide to have "[torn] the place up", turning it into what they called an "economic department" and worked "in a very dispassionate, calm way".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022112_and_114_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022112_and_114-102">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During her two years in the department, Truss launched a ten-year strategy to counter falling bee populations,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatt2014_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatt2014-103">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> approved the limited temporary lifting of a <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a> ban on the use of two <a href="/wiki/Neonicotinoid" title="Neonicotinoid">neonicotinoid</a> <a href="/wiki/Pesticide" title="Pesticide">pesticides</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECarrington2015_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarrington2015-104">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECarrington2012_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarrington2012-105">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> and cut subsidies for <a href="/wiki/Solar_panel" title="Solar panel">solar panels</a> on agricultural land.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Solar_farms_are_a_blight_on_the_landscape,_says_minister&quot;._BBC_News_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Solar_farms_are_a_blight_on_the_landscape,_says_minister&quot;._BBC_News-106">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> In March 2015 Truss was one of two cabinet ministers to vote against the government's proposal to mandate <a href="/wiki/Plain_packaging" class="mw-redirect" title="Plain packaging">plain packaging</a> for cigarettes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPitel2015_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPitel2015-107">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> When she had been asked previously about the issue during a constituency meeting, Truss said "when it comes to things like this, I take a more <a href="/wiki/Libertarian" class="mw-redirect" title="Libertarian">libertarian</a> approach<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>... I don't know if it's the government's role to regulate this".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202272_75-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202272-75">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><span class="anchor" id="That_is_a_disgrace"></span>At the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_Conference_(UK)" title="Conservative Party Conference (UK)">Conservative Party conference</a> in September 2014 Truss made a speech in which she said "we import two thirds of our cheese. That is a disgrace" and "in December, I'll be in Beijing, opening up new pork markets".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWallenfeldt2023_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWallenfeldt2023-108">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022117_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022117-109">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> Four days after Truss delivered the speech, parts of the video were featured on the satirical panel show <i><a href="/wiki/Have_I_Got_News_for_You" title="Have I Got News for You">Have I Got News For You</a></i>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022117_109-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022117-109">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> with the awkward, stilted delivery leading her to be mocked; clips of the speech went viral online.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECole2022_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECole2022-110">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> During the <a href="/wiki/2016_United_Kingdom_European_Union_membership_referendum" title="2016 United Kingdom European Union membership referendum">2016 referendum on leaving the European Union</a>, Truss endorsed <a href="/wiki/Remain_(Brexit)" class="mw-redirect" title="Remain (Brexit)">Remain</a>, saying that the Conservatives had "a golden chance to reform Britain over the next few years" and to avoid "[spending] that time negotiating Britain's exit from the European Union".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarsh2023120_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarsh2023120-111">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022124_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022124-112">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> The referendum resulted in the defeat of Remain and Cameron's resignation; the <a href="/wiki/Home_Secretary" title="Home Secretary">home secretary</a> <a href="/wiki/Theresa_May" title="Theresa May">Theresa May</a> won <a href="/wiki/2016_Conservative_Party_leadership_election" title="2016 Conservative Party leadership election">the ensuing leadership election</a> and subsequently became leader of the Conservative Party and prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Theresa_May_vows_to_be_&#39;one_nation&#39;_prime_minister&quot;._BBC_News_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Theresa_May_vows_to_be_&#39;one_nation&#39;_prime_minister&quot;._BBC_News-113">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Justice_secretary_and_lord_chancellor_.282016.E2.80.932017.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Justice_secretary_and_lord_chancellor_(2016–2017)">Justice secretary and lord chancellor (2016–2017)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liz_Truss&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Justice secretary and lord chancellor (2016–2017)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Official_portrait_of_Elizabeth_Truss_crop_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Truss smiling in front of a light grey background." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Official_portrait_of_Elizabeth_Truss_crop_2.jpg/180px-Official_portrait_of_Elizabeth_Truss_crop_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="240" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Official_portrait_of_Elizabeth_Truss_crop_2.jpg/270px-Official_portrait_of_Elizabeth_Truss_crop_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Official_portrait_of_Elizabeth_Truss_crop_2.jpg/360px-Official_portrait_of_Elizabeth_Truss_crop_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1554" data-file-height="2072" /></a><figcaption>Official portrait, 2017</figcaption></figure>
<p>In July 2016, Truss was appointed as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Justice_(UK)" title="Secretary of State for Justice (UK)">secretary of state for justice</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lord_Chancellor" title="Lord Chancellor">lord chancellor</a> in the <a href="/wiki/First_May_ministry" title="First May ministry">first May ministry</a>, becoming the first female lord chancellor in the office's thousand-year history.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022129_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022129-114">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> May's decision to appoint her was criticised by the <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_(United_Kingdom)" title="Ministry of Justice (United Kingdom)">minister of state for justice</a>, <a href="/wiki/Edward_Faulks,_Baron_Faulks" title="Edward Faulks, Baron Faulks">Edward Faulks</a>, who resigned from the government, questioning whether Truss would "have the clout to be able to stand up to the prime minister when necessary, on behalf of the judges".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGibb2016_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGibb2016-118">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> Other Conservative members of Parliament criticised Truss's appointment owing to her lack of legal experience; in response, Truss's supporters accused one of the MPs, <a href="/wiki/Bob_Neill" title="Bob Neill">Bob Neill</a>, of "thinly veiled misogyny".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022131–132_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022131–132-119">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESyal2016_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESyal2016-120">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Before Truss's arrival, the <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_(United_Kingdom)" title="Ministry of Justice (United Kingdom)">Ministry of Justice</a>'s budget had been subjected to successive cuts under the coalition government, affecting <a href="/wiki/His_Majesty%27s_Prison_Service" title="His Majesty&#39;s Prison Service">prisons</a> and blamed for their rising rates of violence owing to a drop in the number of prison officers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETravis2016b_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETravis2016b-121">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Truss lobbied the <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer" title="Chancellor of the Exchequer">chancellor of the Exchequer</a> <a href="/wiki/Philip_Hammond" title="Philip Hammond">Philip Hammond</a> for £104 million in order to hire an extra 2,500 officers, which Hammond reluctantly delivered.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022132,_135–139_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022132,_135–139-122">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHughes2016_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHughes2016-123">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETravis2016a_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETravis2016a-124">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> In November 2016, Truss was criticised for failing to support the judiciary after three judges of the <a href="/wiki/High_Court_of_Justice" title="High Court of Justice">High Court</a> came under attack from politicians and from the <i><a href="/wiki/Daily_Mail" title="Daily Mail">Daily Mail</a></i>—which ran with the headline "<a href="/wiki/Enemies_of_the_People_(headline)" title="Enemies of the People (headline)">Enemies of the People</a>"—for <a href="/wiki/R_(Miller)_v_Secretary_of_State_for_Exiting_the_European_Union" title="R (Miller) v Secretary of State for Exiting the European Union">ruling against the government</a> on whether <a href="/wiki/Article_50" class="mw-redirect" title="Article 50">Article 50</a>—which would begin the process of leaving the EU—could be triggered without Parliament's approval.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022139–140_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022139–140-125">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> A former lord chancellor, <a href="/wiki/Charlie_Falconer,_Baron_Falconer_of_Thoroton" title="Charlie Falconer, Baron Falconer of Thoroton">Charlie Falconer</a>, suggested that, like her immediate predecessors, Truss lacked legal expertise, called for her to be dismissed as justice secretary as her perceived inadequate response "[signalled] to the judges that they have lost their constitutional protector".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFalconer2016_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFalconer2016-126">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> Truss denied she had failed to defend the judges, writing:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>An independent judiciary is the cornerstone of the rule of law, vital to our constitution and freedoms. It is my duty as lord chancellor to defend that independence. I swore to do so under my oath of office. I take that very seriously, and I will always do so.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEElgot2016_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEElgot2016-127">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span id="Chief_secretary_to_the_Treasury_.282017.E2.80.932019.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Chief_secretary_to_the_Treasury_(2017–2019)">Chief secretary to the Treasury (2017–2019)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liz_Truss&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Chief secretary to the Treasury (2017–2019)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In June, following the <a href="/wiki/2017_UK_general_election" class="mw-redirect" title="2017 UK general election">2017 general election</a>, May demoted Truss from justice secretary to <a href="/wiki/Chief_Secretary_to_the_Treasury" title="Chief Secretary to the Treasury">chief secretary to the Treasury</a>, meaning she could attend cabinet meetings but was not a full member;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAnthony2022_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnthony2022-128">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Truss was enraged and called the demotion "incredibly unfair" and was, according to one of her friends, "seething for a good couple of days".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022151–152_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022151–152-129">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> Despite her reduced confidence from the demotion, Truss rebounded and began to contribute to the department, using it, according to a Treasury worker, "like her own personal think tank" by asking for research and advice on <a href="/wiki/Monetary_policy" title="Monetary policy">monetary policy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022153–154,_156_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022153–154,_156-130">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> In her first few months there, she was largely left out of making decision-making processes as Hammond was, according to Kwarteng, "quite a closed, centrally controlling chancellor"; nevertheless, he and Truss were reported to have a good relationship.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022154–155_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022154–155-131">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> Beginning in December 2017, she developed an enthusiasm for cultivating her presence on <a href="/wiki/X_(social_media_platform)" class="mw-redirect" title="X (social media platform)">Twitter</a> and <a href="/wiki/Instagram" title="Instagram">Instagram</a>; Truss began to plan ministerial visits around <a href="/wiki/Photo_op" title="Photo op">photo ops</a> for her social media.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022156_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022156-132">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECoates2018_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoates2018-133">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETruss2018_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETruss2018-134">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> Some of her civil servants were reported as finding her tenure as chief secretary "exhausting", owing to her work schedule and asking them multiplication questions, a tactic she had first employed as an MP.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202272_75-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale202272-75">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBush2020_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBush2020-135">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Despite her governmental role, Truss remained relatively unknown by the public, with only seven per cent recognising her in March 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022174_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022174-136">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In June 2018 Truss gave a speech criticising what she saw as regulations that got in the way of people's lives, and warned that raising taxes could see the Conservatives being "crushed" at the polls;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECraig2018_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECraig2018-137">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> in particular, she attacked colleagues who she said should realise "that it's not <a href="/wiki/Machismo" title="Machismo">macho</a> just to demand more money",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEElgotCrerar2018_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEElgotCrerar2018-138">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> a jibe at the defence secretary, <a href="/wiki/Gavin_Williamson" title="Gavin Williamson">Gavin Williamson</a>, who had mounted a largely unsuccessful campaign for an extra £20 billion for his department, which included threatening Truss by "writ[ing] to every single British troop and tell[ing] them Liz Truss blocked your pay".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022168–170_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022168–170-139">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> Truss's speech, which also mocked Michael Gove, was criticised by Hammond, the Gove ally <a href="/wiki/Ed_Vaizey" title="Ed Vaizey">Ed Vaizey</a>, and Gove himself;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022170–171_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022170–171-142">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> a speech she gave in November similarly joked about Matt Hancock, the newly appointed home secretary, <a href="/wiki/Sajid_Javid" title="Sajid Javid">Sajid Javid</a>, and the health secretary, <a href="/wiki/Jeremy_Hunt" title="Jeremy Hunt">Jeremy Hunt</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022172_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022172-143">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> Before May's resignation announcement on 24 May 2019, Truss had sought opinion amongst colleagues on whether she could credibly stand, and courted media attention.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERaynerMaidment2019_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERaynerMaidment2019-144">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022174–175_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022174–175-145">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> As it became apparent she could not win, she ruled herself out the day after May announced her resignation and subsequently endorsed the former <a href="/wiki/Foreign_Secretary" title="Foreign Secretary">foreign secretary</a> <a href="/wiki/Boris_Johnson" title="Boris Johnson">Boris Johnson</a>, the first minister to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022175_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022175-146">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="International_trade_secretary_.282019.E2.80.932021.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="International_trade_secretary_(2019–2021)">International trade secretary (2019–2021)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liz_Truss&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: International trade secretary (2019–2021)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Boris_Johnson_official_portrait_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A close-up photograph of Boris Johnson" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/76/Boris_Johnson_official_portrait_%28cropped%29.jpg/180px-Boris_Johnson_official_portrait_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="253" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/76/Boris_Johnson_official_portrait_%28cropped%29.jpg/270px-Boris_Johnson_official_portrait_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/76/Boris_Johnson_official_portrait_%28cropped%29.jpg/360px-Boris_Johnson_official_portrait_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1122" data-file-height="1578" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Boris_Johnson" title="Boris Johnson">Boris Johnson</a>, leader of the Conservative Party from 2019 to 2022<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPayne20224_and_251_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPayne20224_and_251-147">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>After Johnson became prime minister, Truss was tipped for promotion in return for her support during his leadership campaign; it was thought she might have been appointed chancellor or <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Business_and_Trade" title="Secretary of State for Business and Trade">business secretary</a>, but she was instead promoted to the position of <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_International_Trade" title="Secretary of State for International Trade">secretary of state for international trade</a> and <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Board_of_Trade" title="President of the Board of Trade">president of the Board of Trade</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022178_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022178-148">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> Following the resignation of <a href="/wiki/Amber_Rudd" title="Amber Rudd">Amber Rudd</a>, Truss was additionally appointed <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Women_and_Equalities" title="Minister for Women and Equalities">minister for women and equalities</a> in September that year.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGryllsWright2021_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGryllsWright2021-149">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> Shortly after becoming international trade secretary, Truss embarked on international trips to the US, New Zealand, Australia and Japan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022179_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022179-150">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Truss met with her American counterpart, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Lighthizer" title="Robert Lighthizer">Robert Lighthizer</a>, on her first trip to the US, where she gave an "incendiary" speech on a potential US–UK trade deal; in Australia she made unscripted comments on their free trade negotiations with the UK, both events to the dismay of Downing Street officials.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022180_and_185_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022180_and_185-151">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> Truss continued her documentation of trips through her social media.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022182_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeHeale2022182-152">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
